========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, uh… I guess what brings me in is I just can’t seem to sit still when I’m supposed to. It’s like there’s a little motor running under my skin, if that makes sense. Like in staff meetings at work, or even those briefings before church events—everybody’s sitting there listening, and I’m shifting around, tapping, standing up, looking for some reason to go check something or move a chair or whatever.

I mean, I’m a janitor, so moving around is kind of part of my day anyway, and I actually do better when I’m up doing something. But when I have to just sit there, I get real uncomfortable. Like I need to get up and do something, even if there’s nothing that needs doing.

Like I need to get up and do something, even if there’s nothing that needs doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you started or changed the dose of any new medications recently, especially any antipsychotic or anti-nausea drugs, around the time this restlessness began?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Oh, no, this goes way back. I mean, I can remember grade school pretty clearly with that—I was always getting in trouble for popping up out of my seat, or talking before the teacher was done, or starting the worksheet before she’d even finished the directions.

I don’t think there’s been anything new or a dose change that lines up with this.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, would you say you've felt this need to constantly move since you were a kid, or is this something that's only started more recently in your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, this goes way back. I mean, I can remember grade school pretty clearly with that—I was always getting in trouble for popping up out of my seat, or talking before the teacher was done, or starting the worksheet before she’d even finished the directions.\n\nMy mom used to say I was “wound too tight,” which… I guess is kind of funny now, but back then I think she was tired. Um… I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I know I’ll walk into a room and then think, “What did I come in here for?” but doesn’t everybody do that? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yeah, I do that. More than I probably realize.

Like if my supervisor starts telling me what needs done—“after you finish the bathrooms, go check…”—I’ll jump in with, “Oh, the break room trash?” before he’s even finished. And sometimes I’m right, but sometimes I’m wrong and then he has to stop and say, “No, that’s not what I was going to say.”

Same thing at church, if one of the staff is explaining where they want something cleaned or set up, I’ll kind of cut in because I think I know where they’re headed. I don’t mean to be rude. It’s just like my mouth gets there before they’re done talking. Then I feel dumb because they have to correct me.

But when I have to just sit there, I get real uncomfortable. Like I need to get up and do something, even if there’s nothing that needs doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you started or changed the dose of any new medications recently, especially any antipsychotic or anti-nausea drugs, around the time this restlessness began?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I mean, I’m not great at remembering the names of everything, but I haven’t started some big new medicine that I can think of.\n\nAntipsychotic… I don’t think I’m on anything like that. Anti-nausea—no, I mean, I’ve had stomach bugs before, sure, and my wife keeps all kinds of stuff in the bathroom cabinet, but recently? I don’t think there’s been anything new or a dose change that lines up with this.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, would you say you've felt this need to constantly move since you were a kid, or is this something that's only started more recently in your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, this goes way back. I mean, I can remember grade school pretty clearly with that—I was always getting in trouble for popping up out of my seat, or talking before the teacher was done, or starting the worksheet before she’d even finished the directions.\n\nMy mom used to say I was “wound too tight,” which… I guess is kind of funny now, but back then I think she was tired. I’d be bouncing around while she was trying to get supper going, and then I’d be in trouble at school the next day for the same sort of thing. So yeah, it’s not just recent. It’s been there since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself losing focus or drifting off mid-task—like starting a job and forgetting what you were doing, or losing track of instructions—even when you're not moving around?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I know I’ll walk into a room and then think, “What did I come in here for?” but doesn’t everybody do that? My wife says I do it when I’m supposed to grab the trash bags and I come back with, like, a screwdriver because I saw the loose cabinet handle or something.\n\nBut if you mean just sitting there and my mind goes blank… I don’t know. It’s hard to separate it out, because I’m usually up doing something anyway. At work I’ve got my routine—bathrooms, floors, trash, all that—and if someone gives me a long list, I might need them to say it again, but… sorry, I’m kind of wandering. I guess I haven’t really paid attention to whether it’s losing focus or just me getting pulled toward the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Outside of situations where you can move around, like when you're reading paperwork, following written instructions, or doing quiet tasks at home, do you notice yourself making careless mistakes or losing your place because your attention drifts?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Paperwork isn’t a big part of my day, so it’s hard to say. If I’m reading something with a lot of steps, I might go back and check it again, but I don’t know if that’s me losing my place or just wanting to make sure I didn’t miss something.\n\nAt home, quiet stuff… I don’t really sit with quiet tasks for long, honestly. I’ll start looking at a bill or some instructions and then—well, yesterday I was trying to figure out this thing for the sink, and then I remembered the garage light was flickering, so I ended up out there instead. But careless mistakes from my attention drifting? I don’t know. I haven’t really kept track of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting or blurting things out before others finish speaking, or having trouble waiting your turn in conversations or lines?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do that. More than I probably realize.\n\nLike if my supervisor starts telling me what needs done—“after you finish the bathrooms, go check…”—I’ll jump in with, “Oh, the break room trash?” before he’s even finished. And sometimes I’m right, but sometimes I’m wrong and then he has to stop and say, “No, that’s not what I was going to say.”\n\nSame thing at church, if one of the staff is explaining where they want something cleaned or set up, I’ll kind of cut in because I think I know where they’re headed. I don’t mean to be rude. It’s just like my mouth gets there before they’re done talking. Then I feel dumb because they have to correct me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m getting in trouble every day, but it has caused some problems at work.

Like with room turnovers, when we’re trying to get things cleaned and reset fast, I’ll jump ahead because I think I know what they want, and then I miss part of what was actually said. It’s like there’s a little motor running under my skin, if that makes sense. Like in staff meetings at work, or even those briefings before church events—everybody’s sitting there listening, and I’m shifting around, tapping, standing up, looking for some reason to go check something or move a chair or whatever.\n\nI mean, I’m a janitor, so moving around is kind of part of my day anyway, and I actually do better when I’m up doing something. But when I have to just sit there, I get real uncomfortable. Like I need to get up and do something, even if there’s nothing that needs doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you started or changed the dose of any new medications recently, especially any antipsychotic or anti-nausea drugs, around the time this restlessness began?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? I mean, I’m not great at remembering the names of everything, but I haven’t started some big new medicine that I can think of.\n\nAntipsychotic… I don’t think I’m on anything like that. Anti-nausea—no, I mean, I’ve had stomach bugs before, sure, and my wife keeps all kinds of stuff in the bathroom cabinet, but recently? I don’t think there’s been anything new or a dose change that lines up with this.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, would you say you've felt this need to constantly move since you were a kid, or is this something that's only started more recently in your life?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, this goes way back. I mean, I can remember grade school pretty clearly with that—I was always getting in trouble for popping up out of my seat, or talking before the teacher was done, or starting the worksheet before she’d even finished the directions.\n\nMy mom used to say I was “wound too tight,” which… I guess is kind of funny now, but back then I think she was tired. I’d be bouncing around while she was trying to get supper going, and then I’d be in trouble at school the next day for the same sort of thing. So yeah, it’s not just recent. It’s been there since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself losing focus or drifting off mid-task—like starting a job and forgetting what you were doing, or losing track of instructions—even when you're not moving around?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I know I’ll walk into a room and then think, “What did I come in here for?” but doesn’t everybody do that? My wife says I do it when I’m supposed to grab the trash bags and I come back with, like, a screwdriver because I saw the loose cabinet handle or something.\n\nBut if you mean just sitting there and my mind goes blank… I don’t know. It’s hard to separate it out, because I’m usually up doing something anyway. At work I’ve got my routine—bathrooms, floors, trash, all that—and if someone gives me a long list, I might need them to say it again, but… sorry, I’m kind of wandering. I guess I haven’t really paid attention to whether it’s losing focus or just me getting pulled toward the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Outside of situations where you can move around, like when you're reading paperwork, following written instructions, or doing quiet tasks at home, do you notice yourself making careless mistakes or losing your place because your attention drifts?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Paperwork isn’t a big part of my day, so it’s hard to say. If I’m reading something with a lot of steps, I might go back and check it again, but I don’t know if that’s me losing my place or just wanting to make sure I didn’t miss something.\n\nAt home, quiet stuff… I don’t really sit with quiet tasks for long, honestly. I’ll start looking at a bill or some instructions and then—well, yesterday I was trying to figure out this thing for the sink, and then I remembered the garage light was flickering, so I ended up out there instead. But careless mistakes from my attention drifting? I don’t know. I haven’t really kept track of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting or blurting things out before others finish speaking, or having trouble waiting your turn in conversations or lines?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do that. More than I probably realize.\n\nLike if my supervisor starts telling me what needs done—“after you finish the bathrooms, go check…”—I’ll jump in with, “Oh, the break room trash?” before he’s even finished. And sometimes I’m right, but sometimes I’m wrong and then he has to stop and say, “No, that’s not what I was going to say.”\n\nSame thing at church, if one of the staff is explaining where they want something cleaned or set up, I’ll kind of cut in because I think I know where they’re headed. I don’t mean to be rude. It’s just like my mouth gets there before they’re done talking. Then I feel dumb because they have to correct me.\n\nDoctor: Would you say these patterns of restlessness and interrupting have caused real problems for you—like conflicts at work, trouble in your marriage, or other difficulties—because of how they come across to people?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m getting in trouble every day, but it has caused some problems at work.\n\nLike with room turnovers, when we’re trying to get things cleaned and reset fast, I’ll jump ahead because I think I know what they want, and then I miss part of what was actually said. Or with supplies—somebody’s explaining what to use or where to put something, and I cut in or make the decision too quick, and then it turns into friction. Not a huge fight, but people get irritated, and then I get irritated because I feel like they’re talking to me like I’m a kid.\n\nMy supervisor has had to correct me more lately. He actually suggested I talk to the employee assistance program, which… I don’t know, that kind of embarrassed me. I’ve been doing cleaning work a long time, so having someone basically say, “You need to get help with how you’re handling this,” that didn’t feel great. But yeah, I guess that means it’s become a real problem."
  }

Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol). Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYeah… yeah, I do that. More than I probably realize.\n\nLike if my supervisor starts telling me what needs done—“after you finish the bathrooms, go check…”—I’ll jump in with, “Oh, the break room trash?” before he’s even finished. And sometimes I’m right, but sometimes I’m wrong and then he has to stop and say, “No, that’s not what I was going to say.”\n\nSame thing at church, if one of the staff is explaining where they want something cleaned or set up, I’ll kind of cut in because I think I know where they’re headed.   }
